#147. What is ‘texture-maxxing’? The food trend putting crunch on the menu

Texture-maxxing is the latest food trend taking social media by storm as consumers, driven by aesthetics and taste, look for a sensory experience.

British supermarket Waitrose's latest food and drink report highlighted "texture-maxxing" as one of the trends of the year. It describes the phenomenon as a multi-sensory indulgence that is "fuelled by texture-maxxing crunch and intense flavour hits," pushing shoppers to fill their trolleys with crunchy peanut butter, dumplings, and trifles.

"This year, the sound of food went mainstream," Waitrose's Senior Development Chef Will Torrent said in the Wednesday report. "Driven by social media's obsession with audible, multi-sensory cooking, we're creating high-contrast meals that combine shatter-crisp crunch with pillowy chew."

"The emphasis in social media content has flipped from narrative voiceovers to ASMR-inspired audio, where the evocative sounds of food are just as important as the visuals," he added.

Food trends on social media have ranged from the obvious to the absurd. Fibermaxxing and proteinmaxxing have taken off in the past few years. Meanwhile, food and beverages like Dubai chocolate, protein coffee, and ube were some of the big social media hits.

Nestlé's USA 2026 trends found that 75% of Gen Z and 80% of millennials sought out texture in snack cravings with the hashtag "crunchtok," amassing 1.5 billion views.

Waitrose noted that shoppers looking to maximize on texture bought its viral No.1 Alphonso Mango Dessert, which involves "cracking through the crisp shell" to access a "smooth mango mousse and passion fruit curd on a crunchy white chocolate base."

Waitrose's TikTok and Instagram videos of eating the dessert were watched by more than two million people, with a whopping 6,173 hours and 36 minutes of play time on TikTok. Its videos featuring crunchy toast also went viral.

The U.K. grocer's sales of jarred chili crunches, which include crispy rayu oils such as itsu Crispy Chilli Oil, saw a 13-fold jump. Meanwhile, crunchy peanut butter outsold smooth.

Dumplings also had a moment, with sales of chicken gyoza dumplings up 116% this year and searches on waitrose.com for gochujang and peanut butter udon noodles jumped 60%.

"We've seen an explosion in demand as shoppers treat dumplings not just as a quick weeknight bite, but as a total sensory experience," Waitrose's Executive Chef Stephen Parkins-Knight said.

"Driven by the rise of texture-maxxing, people want that ultimate contrast: delicate skins, juicy fillings and crispy skirts, topped with very chilli crunch. With innovation ranging from fusion fillings to rich dipping broths, dumplings have taken a delicious centre stage in 2026," he added.

Texture-maxxing also extended to desserts, as shoppers bought twice as many chocolate trifles compared to 2025, and sales of strawberry trifles increased 160%.

Tiramisu was also popular, with searches for waitrose.com's tiramisu recipe page increasing 155% year-on-year.

#146. New UK-EU treaty has 10-year termination clause

The government and the EU have published the texts of two treaties agreed last week as part of a post-Brexit reset.

The treaties were published on Thursday afternoon, alongside legislation designed to enact the bulk of the deal.

The deal covering food exports has a 10-year termination clause that would mean if one party ended the agreement, they would have to pay "reasonable and proportionate costs" to rebuild border checks infrastructure, which will be significantly scaled back as part of the new deal.

Opposition leader Kemi Badenoch has said she would scrap the deal while Reform UK's Nigel Farage has suggested he would refuse to pay.

The Brexit reset deal has already sparked debate about its benefits, its costs, and a wider discussion on the future of the UK-EU relationship.

The deal covers British food exports, emissions trading and a youth experience scheme.

Two of the three legal texts were released on Thursday, covering the food trade and emissions trading scheme deals, alongside the government's cost-cutting bill to enact the deal.

A new assessment of the impact of the deal on different sectors and regions of the country was also released.

EU offers UK fresh deal on 'Made in Europe' scheme

Badenoch challenges Burnham over university fees for EU students

Farage helps launch new campaign against the UK rejoining the EU

Under the deal, food exporters will be significant beneficiaries from the scrapping of considerable red tape and checks, and dismantling of border facilities. An internal analysis forecasts a 16% boost the export of major agricultural commodities.

The industry and government hope the reduction in costs for food producers and sellers will be passed on to customers, but there may be no clear visible impact at the checkout.

Privately, industry insiders told the BBC earlier this week that there are too many moving parts in the world economy, in particular the energy price shock resulting from war in the Middle East.

Regarding the fine detail in the text of one of the treaties relating to the 10-year termination clause, European affairs minister Hamish Falconer said: "The Cutting Costs Bill allows us to implement two major trade agreements."

"It allows us to eliminate paperwork for our food and drink traders, protect billions of pounds worth of British exports from carbon taxes, and make doing business with our largest trading partner cheaper and easier."

A plan to link British and European emissions trading schemes will see more coverage across the UK's aviation and maritime industries.

The treaty text also contains finer detail of how the UK will observe, shape but not vote on relevant EU rules. The UK will be invited to attend European Commission meetings and expert groups as an observer, as well as taking part in emergency food security planning.

However, the UK will not be given voting rights.

Plans to use secondary legislation, including granting direct so-called "Henry VIII powers" to ministers to align UK and EU laws, are detailed in the legislation.

Such powers will enable ministers to make changes to bills with less Parliamentary scrutiny.

Outlining the agreement with the EU to MPs earlier this week, the prime minister said new agreements with the EU are "pragmatic" and "not a prelude to anything".

Andy Burnham also said it would be "unthinkable" for the UK to rejoin the bloc without a referendum.

Several former prime ministers have urged him not to reopen the Brexit debate. Burnham said they are "stuck in the past" and later claimed there would be "no banging on about Europe" from Labour.

Badenoch said Burnham has "oversold" the deal and was "trying to take Britain back to 2015".

#142. Drivers over 70 face eye tests every three years under new plans

Over-70s will have to take eye tests every three years or face losing their driving licence under new road safety plans.

Mandatory eyesight tests are part of the Department for Transport's (DfT) road safety strategy, expected within weeks.

Around 6.5 million over-70s hold drivers' licences, with 1.6 million of those aged 80 or over.

To drive legally in the UK, you must be able to read a standard number plate from 20 metres away, wearing glasses or contact lenses if required.

Government sources told the BBC ministers will recommend over-70s be made to regularly prove their eyesight meets the minimum standards. Those who do not, or who fail to meet the minimum standard face losing their licence.

The change follows a consultation which launched early last year, set to recommend a shake-up of the rules for older drivers.

Under the current rules, over 70s have to renew their licence every three years, but only have to self-certify that their sight.

Dennis Reed, of campaign group Silver Voices, which represents older people, said it was "discriminatory to just pick an age out of the hat and say 'everybody over 70 has to go through this, but nobody else does'".

But he said the group is not opposed in principle to regular eye tests for older drivers.

The changes being considered were first reported by the Daily Mail, external.

Drivers over 70 to face eye tests every three years

The NHS provides free eye tests for over 60s, but they are not detailed enough to approve someone to drive.

The BBC understands this system is also being looked at, with the free tests likely to be expanded to become more rigorous.

A Government source said "beefed up measures" were long overdue.

"One life lost on the roads is one too many," they added. "There have been several arguably preventable fatalities on Britain's roads caused by drivers with failing eyesight in recent years.

The DfT said no decision has officially been made on mandating eyesight tests for drivers aged 70 and over.

A spokesperson said: "We recognise the importance of driving for older people's independence but need to balance this with our priority to keep everyone safe on our roads and avoid tragic accidents.

"Mandatory eye tests for drivers over 70 as part of our Road Safety Strategy are one way to do this.

"We are carefully reviewing consultation results," they added. "Older drivers who are fit to drive and have good enough vision have nothing to worry about."

#140. Norse Atlantic suspends all flights as it faces potential collapse

The Norwegian low cost carrier Norse Atlantic Airways has suspended all flights as it rushes to resolve what it warned is a critical financial situation.

The airline said if no solution can be found, it intends to file for bankruptcy.

It uses a fleet of 12 Boeing 787 aircraft on long-haul routes from a number of European airports, including London Gatwick, Rome and Oslo.

Prior to the suspension of flights, it was advertising services from London to Orlando, Cape Town, Bangkok and Phuket.

One aircraft was meant to depart London for Orlando this morning, while another was meant to arrive from Cape Town this evening.

The suspension has also affected Pakistan International Airlines, which had been leasing aircraft and crews from Norse Atlantic.

PIA was forced to cancel services from London to Islamabad and from Manchester to Lahore last night.

In a statement on social media, PIA said all UK flights from today would be operated by aircraft from its own fleet.

Londoner Fred was due to fly to Cape Town with Norse Atlantic next week for a friend's wedding, but has decided to change his plans.

"Yesterday the exact same flight I was going to get was cancelled, so I was worried" he told the BBC's Your Voice. "I sent an email to Norse and have had nothing back".

He decided to rebook with a different airline, which cost him an extra Â£1,000.

Another traveller, Claire from Herefordshire, is currently on holiday in South Africa with her daughter.

"We are due to fly home on Sunday and are now looking at a really expensive flight back," she said.

"We have tried contacting Norse but have been given an automated response saying someone will get back to us within three business days."

Consumer group Which? advised Norse Atlantic customers to not cancel their flights as the airline has not collapsed. Doing so removes passengers' right to a refund.

Rory Boland, Which? travel editor, said: "If Norse does go bust, whether you get a refund depends on how you booked and paid. If you booked directly with Norse, you may be able to claim a refund via your credit card provider, or possibly your debit card."

Those stuck abroad should contact insurers, while those with package holidays reliant on Norse Atlantic flights should contact their tour operator, he added.

Norse Atlantic Airways was set up in 2021 as a low-cost long-haul carrier.

It entered the market as a successor to Norwegian, which withdrew from long haul routes as a result of the Covid pandemic.

But it has struggled to make money, and its problems worsened this year, as fuel prices rocketed due to the war in Iran.

Unlike many other European carriers, Norse Atlantic does not hedge its fuel bills by buying supplies in advance at fixed prices.

This left it fully exposed when prices soared, with the cost of fuel roughly doubling at one point. Prices remain significantly higher than they were before the conflict began.

In the first half of the year Norse Atlantic made a loss of $94.6m.

In a statement to the Oslo Stock Exchange on Wednesday, the airline said its financial situation was critical, and it needed to find short term funding to continue its operations.

After suspending flights, the company said it was in discussions with investors regarding a possible takeover that "would include a financing solution".

However, it said if short term funding could not be found quickly, "it is the Board's intention to file for bankruptcy".

Norse Atlantic had been looking for a buyer since July.

Its potential collapse follows the failure of Spirit Airlines in May. The US-based low cost carrier was another airline left exposed when jet fuel prices soared, as US airlines typically do not hedge their purchases.

If Norse Atlantic were to fold, it would be another major setback for the low-cost long-haul model, which has typically proved popular with passengers but difficult to sustain financially, especially on transatlantic routes.

#134. Two Italians killed in Kuwait drone attack; Trump says Iran war ends ‘whenever I want it’

Two Italians have been killed after an alleged Iranian attack on Kuwait, Kuwaiti forces said Thursday.

In a post on X, Kuwait's army said the deaths followed Iranian attacks on the country, with the two Italians killed by falling missile debris. Two others were also injured.

In response, a spokesperson for Iran's military told state media that no offensive or military operations had been carried out in the area and that "these accusations should be viewed as a cover for continued malevolence and an attempt to exacerbate insecurity in the region" by U.S. and Israeli forces.

Iranian Foreign Minister Abbas Araghchi said on Telegram that he had spoken with his Italian counterpart, Antonio Tajani, and expressed regret over their deaths, while stressing that Tehran had no role or involvement in the incident.

"The incident is suspicious and looks like a precooked recipe for war," Araghchi wrote on X.

He also said Iran was ready to cooperate with the governments of Kuwait and Italy in investigating the incident.

Separately, Israel said Thursday that it was prepared to carry out strikes against Iran "at any moment," even without U.S. forces joining the effort, according to Israeli military spokesman Effie Defrin on Israeli broadcaster Kan.

The comments came after U.S. envoy Jared Kushner told a podcast Wednesday that Israel "needs to get out of the constant wartime mindset."

Israel has been pushing the Trump administration to back another offensive against Tehran, with Reuters reporting on Nov. 18 that Israeli spy chief Roman Gofman pushed for joint strikes when he met U.S. officials earlier in the month.

Late Thursday, President Donald Trump said on PBS that the U.S. had "victory at hand" in the Iran conflict and reiterated that victory would come "whenever I want it to be, to be honest."

Speaking during a Thanksgiving call with U.S. military personnel, Trump said Washington was still trying to give Iran "every chance to make a deal and save a lot of lives."

But he added that he was not sure whether Tehran would agree.

"Some people question why. Are they crazy? Maybe they are," Trump said.

#133. When will Poundland shops close? Five key questions answered

Poundland has collapsed into administration, leaving the jobs of its 11,000 staff in doubt in the run-up to Christmas.

The company has appointed consultancy Alix Partners to oversee a sale of part or all of the discount retailer, with questions remaining about the future of the company.

This is the question that is most difficult to answer.

Poundland shops will remain open as usual for now. The firm collapsing has acted as a reset in the process of selling it.

Administrators are looking to either sell the business as a whole, or to break it up, which is the more likely outcome.

When Poundland was still a going concern, Irish property firm Martin Property Group made an offer for it. But a source said the group was now "unlikely" to make a fresh bid due to "irreparable harm" to the brand from the administration process.

Before the collapse, Fortress, which owns rival budget chain Poundstretcher, was interested in buying about 200 of the stores, and wanted to keep those trading under the Poundland brand.

But Fortress's position at the moment is unclear. The BBC understands it is considering selling Poundstretcher, a move first reported by Bloomberg.

A decision on Poundstretcher could come before the end of this week. However, Fortress has declined to comment.

When the chain was sold by Pepco last year for Â£1 it was struggling. It suffered a Â£68m loss that year.

Poundland's managing director Barry Williams said Poundland had been seeing a "strong recovery" and recorded a profit by one measure in the first nine months of this year.

Poundland's management paint a picture of a retailer that was turning a corner until the owner, US investment firm Gordon Brothers, pulled the plug on funding for day-to-day expenses such as buying stock.

The Usdaw union, which represents shopworkers, claim the owners forced Poundland into administration.

Labour MP Antonia Bance said she was "pretty appalled" by the conduct of Gordon Brothers over the past few weeks, and that it was going to make much bigger profits by breaking up the business rather than by keeping it going.

However, Gordon Brothers insisted the business was only turning a profit on paper, and had missed budget forecasts.

A spokeswoman said while the management team had made Â£100m in cost reductions, the business had moved from a Â£70m loss to "around break-even".

"Break-even is different from generating the cash needed to buy stock, invest, and withstand increasing economic pressures," the spokeswoman said. "A few good weeks do not make a turnaround."

Poundland repeatedly missed budget targets over the year, the spokeswoman added.

Gordon Brothers was concerned that as inflation goes up next year, those pressures would have helped to make the business unviable, it said.

And Julie Palmer, managing partner at insolvency agency Begbies Traynor, said Poundland's collapse "seemed somehow inevitable" due to challenges facing retailers - including higher costs to employ staff.

Stores are still trading while the administrators work out if the business is viable.

The funds locked up by Gordon Brothers - at the last count about Â£70m - have now been released by the administrators.

This has turned the taps back on, so staff will continue to be paid in the run-up to Christmas, and so will suppliers.

But here's the catch: the administrators have a legal obligation to maximise value for "secured" creditors. It means the owners, Gordon Brothers, are at the head of the queue for being paid back the loans that were stumbling blocks in the negotiations.

But more importantly for staff, if the administrators can get more value by breaking up the business than by selling it as a whole, then that is what they have to do, even if the business would be viable.

If the business is broken up and its stores are sold off, the new owners would not be obliged to keep staff on.

From 26 November you will not get a refund for goods if you change your mind about the purchase. If the item is faulty you will still get a refund.

The immediate reason Poundland collapsed was that Gordon Brothers locked up funds for the day-to-day running of the business, including to pay suppliers.

This meant that stock was running low at Poundland.

But those funds have now been unlocked, meaning suppliers can be paid, and supply chains can be re-established, at least in the short term.

If an item is on the shelves, then shoppers can buy it.

Administrators will have to get the best value for creditors, which could mean selling the business as it stands.

But if Poundland is broken up, administrators could sell the brand without the stores, or the brand along with some of the stores, or the stores without the Poundland brand, to other retailers.

As a result there could be fewer discount stores on the High Street, providing less incentive for other retailers to keep prices down.

"Poundland is a place they go on the High Street to get bargains and... given the cost of living challenges we face, that is of concern to us in government," Wes Streeting, the defence secretary, told the BBC on Thursday.

Poundland's customers may well turn to rival budget retailers in the hunt for bargains.

Are you a Poundland employee? Tell us your story. Get in touch.

#131. Macy's postpones Thanksgiving Day Parade due to severe weather

Macy's on Wednesday announced it's postponing its Thanksgiving Day Parade, originally scheduled for Thursday, due to severe weather.

The parade, held each year on Thanksgiving morning, will now take place on Saturday at 8:30 a.m. ET, according to the company. The New York City area is expected to have heavy rain and thunderstorms on Thanksgiving.

"The safety of our participants and spectators is our top priority, and after close consultation with our city and agency partners, we have made the decision to move this year's celebration," Macy's said in a statement.

This year's event marks the 100th Macy's Thanksgiving Day Parade. The annual event has never been canceled due to weather, though it was suspended between 1942 and 1944 for World War II.

The parade's viability this year had been in question after concerns arose over strong winds that could become dangerous for the massive balloons that the event is known for. Many of the balloons had already been inflated as of Wednesday afternoon.

The postponement also means thousands of volunteers and artists slated to perform will have to adapt to Saturday's schedule.

#130. New boss appointed to lead Travelodge

Peter Krueger will lead Travelodge after a turbulent few months

Travelodge has appointed a former Tui executive to become its new boss from the start of December.

The former CEO of the Oxfordshire-based budget hotel chain Jo Boydell quit in the summer after safety issues were raised at many of its sites.

A woman staying at its Maidenhead hotel was sexually assaulted in December 2022 after a man lied to staff to get a key card to her room. Kyran Smith was jailed for seven-and-a-half years for the attack.

Peter Krueger will start his new role at Travelodge on Tuesday, replacing interim chief Ray Reidy who has led the company since Boydell quit.

She left the company after facing significant criticism over the handling of the sexual assault incident and wider safety concerns.

It prompted former prime minister Sir Keir Starmer to urge her to "seriously engage" with the government on the issue after she cancelled a meeting with MPs.

An independent review by Paul Greaney KC published in October found the sexual assault victim was treated in a "disgraceful" way and "fundamentally failed" by the hotel chain.

In an update on Thursday, Reidy said the business is "continuing to make further investments into room security, and the safety and security of our guests remains our top priority".

Travelodge said it will be making additional investments in room security "over the course of 2027".

#128. It’s not just you: Durable goods are getting less reliable, from sputtering cars to glitching smart TVs

"They just don't make things like they used to." The phrase, formerly exclusive to grandparents, is now used by consumers of all ages.

Across the United States, durable goods like cars, appliances and electronics are becoming less reliable. In the automobile industry, owners of 3-year-old models reported an average of 204 problems per 100 vehicles as of Feb. 12, the highest level since 2022, according to a report from consumer intelligence company J.D. Power. Reliability is a growing concern for dishwashers and front-load washers too, respectively experiencing an average of 92 and 91 problems per 100 units as of Oct. 29, according to a similar J.D. Power report.

Consumers have noticed: Shoppers dealing with high inflation rates and added tariffs on imported goods are unsatisfied with all kinds of products, from cars and tech devices to furniture and kitchen gear. Just over three-quarters of Americans say they believe companies often sell lower-quality products and services at higher prices, according to an Axios and Harris Poll survey published in May 2025.

To be sure, reliability and durability aren't the only ways to define a product's quality. Aesthetics and performance matter to consumers, too. Your television today may not last as long as the one you grew up with, but it's probably larger and thinner with better picture definition — and it likely costs much less, when adjusted for inflation, says Justin Sirotin, an industrial design professor at the Rhode Island School of Design. Indeed, the average price of a television was roughly $622 in 1996 adjusted for inflation, whereas a 55-inch LCD model now costs $232 on average.

But because they're penny-pinched, consumers are becoming more focused on finding high-value products — ones that perform consistently and reliably — than on price, according to a 2025 Deloitte report.

Unfortunately for shoppers, some businesses are also economically squeezed, and may manufacture products with cheaper, less durable parts to keep their profits growing, Sirotin says. Plus, we've all gotten used to sleek products with impressive features at affordable prices, and when businesses have to deliver on those aspects to grow demand, longevity can drop down the list of priorities. Manufacturers may prioritize quality on the external parts of a product — like the picture quality on a television — and get their cost savings with cheaper internal components that you won't initially notice, says Keyana Sapp, an investigative journalist and creator of the Worse on Purpose newsletter.

In some cases, a product's durability may even be part of a "planned obsolescence," wherein businesses intentionally give goods a certain lifespan so customers need to buy replacements within a predictable timeframe. Planned obsolescence isn't necessarily rampant, and it's hard to prove that any individual company purposefully designs products to fail, Sapp says.

But historically speaking, it's not entirely a conspiracy theory. In 1924, major light bulb manufacturers formed an international coalition and agreed to lower and standardize the lifespan of incandescent light bulbs to 1,000 hours. The cartel broke up during World War II.

You can, of course, try extending the longevity of products you've already bought by paying to repair them, rather than getting a brand-new replacement. But as devices like vacuum cleaners and headphones evolve technologically, they require increasingly specialized tools and labor, says Shahram Mokhtari, lead teardown technician at online repair community and e-commerce site iFixit. Specialized screws, glued components and limited access to repair information can make some products difficult or impossible to fix, the Federal Trade Commission reported in May 2021.

Europe has right-to-repair regulations that require manufacturers of certain products to provide access to replacement parts, tools and information. Only nine U.S. states have similar legislation for consumer electronics.

If you want to know how long a product might last before you buy it, look up its reviews or do some research to understand if it's complicated to fix, Mokhtari recommends. Or, if you have the budget, you can prioritize higher-priced products that might bring both initial quality and long-term longevity. This may require intentional long-term planning for some consumers — even as online shopping has made impulse buying easier, Sirotin notes.

"It takes a lot of intentionality by the consumer to say, 'OK, well, is this chair really a chair that I want to have for 25 years? Or is this the chair I'm going to buy and sit in for six months, and then I'm just going to get another chair?'" says Sirotin.

#127. Millions of people to see average monthly mortgage payments rise by £100

More people face higher mortgage repayments when they roll off a fixed deal than previously estimated, new figures from the the Bank of England show.

About 6.5 million owners are forecast to see their monthly repayment jump by Â£100 on average when they move onto their next deal by the end of 2029, new data in its Financial Stability Report reveals.

That is more the double the Â£45-a-month hike that the Bank estimated in its last report in July.

In the shorter term, about one million of those homeowners, who are on mortgage rates below 3% and due to refinance by the end of next year, will see an average Â£200 increase in their monthly repayments.

For borrowers, the interest rate on a fixed mortgage does not change until it expires, usually after two or five years, and a new one is chosen to replace it. The vast majority of homeowners and buyers have this kind of mortgage.

Since the Iran war began, global economic uncertainty has been pushing up the cost of deals, with the average rates on two and five year fixed mortgages at their highest for about three years.

On Thursday, the average rate on a two-year deal was 5.99%, and at 6% for a five-year deal, according to the financial information service Moneyfacts.

Analysts have described the situation as "brutal" for homeowners coming to the end of their fixed deals.

However, the Bank's report points out that the average Â£100 increase in monthly repayments was half the rise experienced by those refinancing during 2023 and 2024.

Dawn Fazackerley-King is a Pilates instructor who lives near Ipswich and has previously spoken to the BBC about her struggles with the cost of living.

She was on a five-year fixed deal at 1.4%, which she took out in January 2022. But the mother-of-two is about to refix for two years and the best deal she can find is a 5.69% loan. That will push her costs up by about Â£200 a month.

Interest rates fluctuate based on the Bank of England's base rate and market conditions

She is far from alone, with one million homeowners facing a similar increase often because they are coming off deals that were agreed before interest rates began rising in 2022.

The Bank's report said that households facing higher payments "may need to reduce discretionary spending or draw down savings as higher energy and other essential costs feed through".

Most had savings buffers but lower-income households, including renters, had less wriggle room owing to spending a greater proportion of their income on essentials.

Early in November, the Bank raised the benchmark interest rate, which influences borrowing costs for individuals and businesses, for the first time in more than three years, from 3.75% to 4%.

The numbers could change were mortgage rates to fall, primarily if international economic volatility eased.

Even so, the Bank said around 350,000 were already projected to see monthly mortgage repayments fall when they fixed their next deal within three years.

The report also suggested there were no signs of an increase in borrowers defaulting on their loans, while UK households remained resilient.

"We have not seen the scale of mortgage arrears that we saw if you go back to earlier periods, the early 90s, the early 80s, actually even the financial crisis. And we are not seeing the scale of house repossessions, and that has to be a good thing," said the Bank's governor, Andrew Bailey.

However, separately, the Bank warned that cyber attacks had become a more prominent threat to the UK's financial stability.

Financial firms needed to "keep pace with the growing scale and sophistication of cyber threats", the report said.

#126. 'The Traitors' ended with a $246,300 prize—here's who won and what they told us about spending it

When Shane Beatty, a contestant on NBC's "The Traitors: New Blood," deceived his fellow finalists, he walked away with the entire $246,300 prize. But the emotional toll of winning caught him by surprise.

"The second I had to reveal that I was a Traitor, everything hit me at once," Beatty, a 32-year-old ironworker from Staten Island, New York, tells CNBC Make It. "I had built genuine relationships with these people, and knowing that some of them were blindsided by my decision was difficult."

Now that he's won, Beatty has a plan for his money, starting with setting aside a sizable share for Uncle Sam. As for the rest, he's more interested in financial security than the trappings of newfound wealth.

After winning, Beatty doesn't expect his day-to-day life to change much.

"I'm still going to get up, go to work, and continue doing what I've been doing as a Local 580 ironworker," he says. "I love my career, and winning this money doesn't change that."

Beatty plans to pay for a wedding he and his fiancée previously struggled to afford, help his mother pay down debt and clear his own debts before saving the rest.

"What it does give me is an opportunity to take some financial pressure off, be a little more comfortable and put myself in a better position for the future," he says.

But before spending his winnings, Beatty is accounting for what could be his single biggest expense — taxes.

"First thing I'm gonna do with the money is put at least $100,000 in a separate bank account just so I can have it ready for Uncle Sam," he told Entertainment Weekly last week.

Setting aside $100,000 is a reasonable move given Beatty's potential tax liability, according to tax experts.

"Taxes are often an afterthought when winning big on a gameshow," says Riley Adams, a certified public accountant and founder of Sequoia Ridge CPAs. "So, it's definitely smart on his part to get ahead with his tax planning."

Game show winnings are generally taxed as ordinary income, meaning the prize money is added to a winner's other taxable income for the year. That can push a winner into higher tax brackets.

In addition to federal income taxes, Beatty, who lives in New Jersey, will have to account for state income taxes.

While Beatty did not disclose his income to CNBC Make It, ironworkers in the New York City metropolitan area typically earn around $100,000 a year, according to 2025 Bureau of Labor Statistics data.

Assuming Beatty lives in New Jersey, earns $100,000 from his job in New York in 2026, receives his winnings this year, files as a single taxpayer with no dependents and takes the standard deduction, here's how much of his prize money he could keep, according to Adams' calculations:

Under this scenario, Beatty's winnings would push him from the 22% federal income tax bracket into the 35% bracket. Factoring in state income taxes, an estimated 35% of his prize would go to taxes, according to Adams' calculations. Setting aside $100,000 would give Beatty a comfortable cushion under this scenario. His actual tax bill, however, will depend on his income, deductions and other financial circumstances.

Jim Guarino, a certified public accountant and managing director at Baker Newman Noyes in Woburn, Massachusetts, arrived at a similar estimate.

"Folks tend to get excited by the 'gross amount' of their total winnings," but Beatty is "extremely tax-aware" in recognizing how much less he'll actually get to keep after taxes, he says.

For Beatty, the money won't radically change his life, but it will give him greater financial security.

"Having a savings cushion and knowing I don't have to worry about living paycheck to paycheck or falling behind on bills means so much to me," he says. "I want to be responsible with this opportunity and make sure it gives me greater financial peace of mind."

#125. The Tech Download: Will Jensen Huang’s return be the spark the shaky AI market needs?

The recent Nvidia saga has had more twists and turns than an Agatha Christie novel.

It all started with Nvidia co-founder and CEO Jensen Huang's admission that he had been unfaithful to his wife. Days later, amid an independent board investigation and broader scrutiny over national-security concerns, he resigned. Less than two weeks later, Huang was back at the helm.

During all of that, Nvidia stock lost over $1 trillion and then regained just under $1 trillion.

The leadership drama has played out at a time when the artificial intelligence market is looking shakier than it has in some time.

Neoclouds have seen stocks plummet in recent months as investors question the sustainability of the AI boom. Chip stocks like Arm and AMD have both fallen in the past month.

Huang's surprise return saw Nvidia stock surge 31%, with other AI stocks also gaining. But the sheen was off by Tuesday's close, with Nvidia and a host of chip stocks falling.

The sharp reversal raises questions for investors: did Huang's return restore confidence in Nvidia, or did it spark a short-lived relief rally as the market faces broader questions about returns on AI investment?

Judging by the first few days of his return, it's a hard one to call.

Volatility in the tech sector looked set to continue on Friday, as tech stocks rebounded again.

"Wall Street pared its Monday advance as sobriety returned to the market and a focus returned to fundamentals," wrote Kyle Rodda, analyst at Capital.com, in a note at market close on Tuesday.

"While Jensen Huang's reinstatement as Nvidia CEO is indubitably a boon for the markets and the overall AI trade, it does not assuage every risk and concern about an industry confronting a potential peak in its investment boom and moderating earnings growth."

The early week rally was "largely driven" by Huang's return, said Violeta Todorova, senior research analyst at Leverage Shares.

"Fresh tariff threats from the Trump administration have added to market uncertainty," she added. "The short-term rally has reversed course, and Nvidia's share price is likely to trade sideways between $125 and $170 in the near term."

Nvidia stock closed at $149.30 on Wednesday, giving it a $3.6 trillion market cap — a figure around $2 trillion lighter than it was at its peak in October.

"We expect a selective recovery across the AI ecosystem, and NVDA to trade sideways and stay volatile below its October peak at least until the Dec 1–3 Congressional appearances," Egor Tolmachev, senior analyst at Freedom Capital Markets, said in a note on Wednesday.

Other key AI stocks have also been volatile. Anthropic's first week as a public company has been mixed. AMD has fallen 25% in the past five days, and memory chip maker Micron has dropped 18%. Both spiked on Monday before paring gains.

The Huang evangelists might've been hoping for a speedier return to AI boom times.

But while the Nvidia CEO's return has removed one source of uncertainty around the chip giant, the volatility among AI stocks suggests investors are still trading on questions much bigger than who occupies the chief executive's office.

Tech German software major SAP is reportedly weighing cutting at least 10,000 jobs in AI push.

U.S. adds Megaspeed to trade blacklist over export control concerns amid Nvidia controversy.

Robots could be doing household chores in just 1-2 years, a Chinese EV firm says.

SpaceX, Musk pledge to donate a share each to active Trump Accounts.

Jensen Huang tells staff that Nvidia doesn't need a succession plan, according to people inside the room.

Jensen Huang is back as the CEO of Nvidia, and is now tasked with selling the company's AI vision while also defending himself in Washington, my colleague Kif Leswing wrote on Tuesday.

#124. Spain’s election could pull Madrid closer to Trump’s Washington

Spanish voters head to the polls on Sunday for a snap election that could bring down socialist Prime Minister Pedro Sánchez, who has repeatedly clashed with U.S. President Donald Trump.

It could also put the far right in government for the first time in the country's modern democratic era.

The center-right Popular Party is the frontrunner, and it could win a majority with far-right Vox, according to El País's final polling averages. A government led by the PP could shift Spain's approach to Washington, Beijing and NATO.

It's the first test of whether Europe's mainstream conservatives are willing to bring the far right into government, ahead of elections in France and Poland next year.

Despite Spain's economy growing 2.7% year over year in the third quarter, more than twice the euro zone's 1.3%, the campaign has been dominated by housing and cost-of-living crises.

Inflation soared to 4.9% in the year to October, above a euro zone average of 4%. November's flash estimate published Friday showed Spanish inflation cooling to 4.1%, still well above the 2.3% reading at the start of the year. In an October YouGov survey, 53% of respondents named housing one of the country's two most important issues.

Home purchase prices nearly doubled in the decade to the second quarter of 2026, according to Eurostat. In 2025, house prices rose nearly three times as fast as household incomes, the European Commission said.

Nationwide protests began after an 87-year-old woman died two weeks after being evicted from her Madrid apartment in September.

Sánchez called the snap election after lawmakers rejected two government decrees designed to broaden housing protections, including limiting rent increases and blocking certain evictions and automatic lease renewals. Congress later ratified a revised version of one decree, limiting rent increases and evictions in some circumstances.

Sánchez has made a name for himself internationally after clashing with Trump over NATO spending, the war in Gaza and Spain's refusal to let U.S. forces use its bases for operations against Iran. Trump even threatened to cut off "all trade" with Spain during the dispute over the bases.

At a Nov. 21 rally in Tenerife, Sánchez asked: "What good has President Trump brought to the United States and, by extension, to the world?"

He has also told voters that the PP and Vox would roll back government cost-of-living assistance. Days before the election, Sánchez's government approved 1.21 billion euros ($1.33 billion) to extend public transport discounts through 2027 and approved a general pay increase of up to 4.5% for 2027.

But in a final pre-election survey for El Español on Nov. 23, 66% of respondents gave Sánchez a failing grade and 29.5% a passing one. His government has faced scrutiny over its handling of the Ceuta migrant crisis, two deadly train collisions this year and corruption cases involving people close to the prime minister.

PP leader Alberto Núñez Feijóo, the current favorite to replace Sánchez, has made alleged government corruption and inflation central to his campaign. At a Nov. 22 rally in Murcia, he said the government wanted voters to forget "that inflation is eating into your pay check, that you can't afford a home, that you're paying more than ever for everything."

The PP led the final El País polling average, published Nov. 24, with 31.4%, followed by Sánchez's Socialists at 29.1% and Vox at 17.9%. Several seat projections put the PP and Vox above the 176 seats needed for a majority in the 350-seat lower house. If Vox joins a government, it would be the first far-right party to do so since Spain returned to democracy after Francisco Franco, the dictator who ruled for 40 years, died in 1975.

The PP wants to repeal the 2023 housing law and scrap rent caps in designated high-pressure areas. It proposes speeding up construction permits and lowering taxes on first homes for buyers under 40. Vox also wants to repeal the housing law and would prioritize Spanish citizens for subsidized housing and public services.

Sánchez's Socialists want to extend renter protections and make at least a quarter of new homes public or permanently price-restricted. It would use proceeds from a bank levy to support housing, which it says will raise a further $1.5 billion. The PP has pledged to scrap this.

The PP also favors easing climate rules, extending the life of nuclear plants and replacing a decree that sets tight new renewable-power requirements on data centers with large new grid connections.

A PP-Vox coalition would have its tensions, with immigration likely to be a major pressure point as Vox has an increasingly hard line on deportation and the militarization of some borders. The parties clashed in regional governments over the distribution of unaccompanied migrant children, leading Vox to quit several coalitions in 2024.

Immigration has since gained more prominence amid the government's handling of reports that more than 70,000 people crossed from Morocco into the Spanish enclave of Ceuta on July 30 and 31.

"Immigration has played a significant role in supporting Spain's recent growth, a strong role for Vox could complicate the government's ability to sustain that," said Jess Middleton, senior Europe analyst at risk intelligence company Verisk Maplecroft.

The parties also differ on defense commitments: both support more investment, but neither has committed to NATO's target of spending 5% of GDP on defense. The PP pledges closer NATO cooperation, while Vox puts greater emphasis on border security.

The PP favors closer integration with the European Union, while Vox wants more independence from it. The success or failure of a PP-Vox partnership will also have wider lessons for Europe "where long-standing firewalls against far-right parties are coming under strain," added Middleton, pointing to the rise of Germany's AfD and the prospect of Marine Le Pen potentially winning next year's election in France.

A shift to the right could improve relations between the U.S. and Spain. Sánchez used his tenure to strengthen ties with Beijing, the country's largest goods trading partner outside the EU.

The U.S. remained Spain's largest source of foreign direct investment. Flows more than doubled to 11.6 billion euros in the 12 months through June 2026, from 5.1 billion euros in the previous 12 months, according to Spain's Economy Ministry.

Carla Hobbs, head of the European Council on Foreign Relations' Madrid office, expects a PP-led government to seek closer ties with Washington and bring its China policy more in line with the EU's. Vox could complicate that. The first test for Washington would be whether a new government changes Spain's position on defense spending or the use of its bases.

#122. Simon Jack: Is Poundland's US owner to blame for its collapse?

Watch: Why has Poundland collapsed into administration?

The blame game for the demise of Poundland is in full swing.

The number one target is US-based Gordon Brothers who bought the discount chain for Â£1 and have taken nearly Â£20m out of the business already.

Gordon Brothers stand to get paid back a further Â£22.5m, as their share of an existing Â£30m loan is set to be one of the first debts that the administrators will have to pay out of the proceeds of any sale of the business in whole or in part.

The team running Poundland blame the owners for strangling their ability to turn the business around, saying that their access to Poundland's own cash was restricted, leaving them unable to fully restock ahead of the crucial Christmas period.

They point to improving sales over recent months as proof the business had a future.

MPs also laid into the US firm with members of the Business Committee accusing it of "predatory capitalism" and "asset stripping".

Poundland collapses putting 11,000 jobs at risk

No-one appears to dispute the fact that Gordon Brothers' insistence that they get paid most of their Â£22.5m debt was a major stumbling block to a sale.

Potential buyers balked at paying out millions on a loan that Gordon Brothers inherited for nothing.

Martin Property Group offered Â£5m up front with the balance to be deferred, while Xyra Holdings offered Â£12.5m up front but, according to sources close to the deal, were thought unable to actually pay.

Unsurprisingly perhaps, Gordon Brothers dispute a lot of how they have been characterised.

First, they insist that they lent the business Â£85m to try to keep it alive for the 17 months since they bought it. Without them, this collapse would have happened in June last year.

The Â£20m taken out of the business since then has mostly been interest on that loan.

Despite brief periods of sales growth compared with a year earlier, Poundland was not generating enough cash to sustain itself. Sales were forecast to be Â£115m lower than predicted when Gordon Brothers bought the business - more than offsetting a Â£100m cost-cutting programme.

Like other retailers Poundland was hit by rising labour costs, employment taxes and business rates.

In the end, argue Gordon Brothers, none of the potential buyers could meet their terms and prove they had the money and expertise to make a go of it.

Such is the discrepancy between the two versions that the chair of the Business Committee wants Gordon Brothers and the management back in front of them to get to the truth.

It is still possible that a bidder may emerge in the coming hours and days to save the business as a whole but most industry observers think that looks unlikely.

Julie Palmer of insolvency specialists Begbies Traynor told the BBC: "This sort of direction of travel seemed somehow inevitable. Obviously, if one of those interested parties steps up and buys the whole business and assets, that's the best, quickest and cleanest outcome. I think realistically, though, there is a chance now that there will be a breakup."

Like previous busts such as Woolworths, BHS and Wilko, many of these stores will find new owners and they will need local, experienced staff.

But none of that will be much comfort to those workers facing grave uncertainty a month before Christmas, when they should be at their busiest and most needed.

Gordon Brothers specialise in situations of commercial distress. When they take on a distressed company, they try to make sure that the worst possible outcome is still one that makes money for them.

In this situation, the worst outcome would mean they get paid back Â£22.5m, earn interest on the loan they provided the company (secured against the company assets) and a few other miscellaneous fees.

Little wonder then that many people say they had little incentive to strain every sinew to secure a buyer. They argue they kept a business that would have collapsed last June going for another 17 months, which extended employment for thousands of workers.

But if, as some retail veterans have told the BBC, Gordon Brothers never really intended to turn this business around, why would the previous owners PepCo give them a no-lose gamble? Why didn't they do what Gordon Brothers are doing - profiting with no risk?

The answer is simple: Pepco have other businesses, which could have got tangled up in the administration of Poundland.

#121. New car prices are at a record high. Here's where to look for deals

Consumers who are in the market for a new car may find the latest pricing data somewhat sobering.

The average transaction price for a new vehicle reached an all-time high of $51,109 in October, up 2.1% from a year earlier, according to a report from Kelley Blue Book, an auto valuation company. The amount excludes incentives, taxes and fees.

But for consumers who can't spend that much — or don't want to — the good news is that plenty of buyers are shelling out much less for a vehicle.

"The average is telling us as much about product mix as it is about affordability," Erin Keating, executive analyst at Cox Automotive, which owns Kelley Blue Book, said in the report.

Of the five highest-volume car types sold in October, three — compact and subcompact SUVs, and compact cars — sold at prices far below the overall average transaction price, she said. For example, the average amount paid for a compact car in October was $28,030.

Additionally, sales of vehicles priced from about $28,000 to $38,000 climbed 9.6% from a year earlier.

"Consumers are still buying plenty of vehicles well below the headline average transaction price, even as strong demand for full-size pickups and other larger, well-equipped vehicles keeps pushing the average to new highs," Keating said.

The record price comes amid a 34% jump in the average cost of a new vehicle since October 2019, when it was $38,259, according to Kelley Blue Book data.

While pandemic-related supply shortages helped drive up prices earlier this decade, consumers' appetite for larger, more expensive vehicles like SUVs and pickup trucks has also pushed the average higher. Full-size pickups, for instance, sold for an average of $67,912 in October, according to Kelley Blue Book.

Yet buyers who would struggle to afford a $50,000-plus new car can still spend less and find deals in the current market.

For starters, automakers have been slower than usual to roll out 2027 models, at least in part because 2026 cars continue to sell well at current prices, said Ivan Drury, director of insights at Edmunds, an automotive research company. In October, 76% of new-vehicle sales were for current-year models, compared with 59% a year earlier.

This slowdown in the annual model-year transition means more 2027 cars will hit dealer lots soon, which suggests dealers and automakers are motivated to clear 2026 inventory, Drury said.

But waiting until the last week of the year to buy — thinking dealerships are desperate to make the sale — won't necessarily get you a better deal, he said.

"You're not going to get massive savings by waiting," Drury said. "Everybody's busiest time of the year is between Christmas and New Year's, but it's not like the best deals are then — that's a perception thing."

Some manufacturers are offering incentives on certain 2026 models, which can reduce the cost of a new car by thousands of dollars.

For example, the 2026 Nissan Pathfinder, a midsize SUV with a starting price of $41,565 — which includes destination charges — comes with a rebate of $5,000 for qualified buyers through Nov. 30, according to Cars.com, an online automotive marketplace.

Just be sure to check your local dealerships to confirm participation for any manufacturer's incentives, experts say.

Look for cars that have been sitting on dealer lots for an extended time, Drury said. In September, new cars took 58 days to sell, on average, according to Edmunds.

Generally, dealerships consider cars unsold for 60 to 90 days to be aging inventory, which may motivate them to offer a better deal, according to Autotrader.com, an online auto marketplace owned by Cox Automotive.

"You'll find the vehicles that dealers definitely want to get rid of the most," Drury said. Looking for older inventory will be "your quickest way to find a dealership that's thinking, 'Yes, I too want to get rid of this. Help me help you,'" he said.

Listings on some car-shopping websites include how long a car has been on a dealership's lot. You can also ask the dealership how long a particular car has been in its inventory.

Leasing may present an opportunity to get into a car with lower monthly payments than financing the same vehicle, depending on the terms of the lease.

However, fewer attractive leasing deals are available, a trend that started during the pandemic, Drury said. Plus, leases, which are typically for three years, usually come with mileage limits and charges for excessive wear and tear.

Nevertheless, leasing may make financial sense for a consumer who "gets that two- or three-year itch for a new car," Drury said.

This is because after the first two or three years of making payments on a car loan — a time period when the steepest depreciation generally occurs — the car's value may be less than what is still owed on the loan, depending on the specific terms. This is known as being upside down or underwater, or having negative equity. In that case, the amount must either be paid off or added to the buyer's new loan.

"We see so many cars traded in that are 3 years old, and the customer is upside down by $7,000, $8,000 or $10,000 and they roll that into another loan," Drury said.

You also may be able to find a good deal on a used car that's not too old.

The average transaction price for a 3-year-old vehicle was $32,039 in the third quarter, down slightly from $32,461 in the second quarter but 3.1% higher than $31,067 a year earlier, according to Edmunds. It's also higher than the average $27,133 list price for used cars of any age in October, according to Cox.

However, these 3-year-old cars are selling for far less than they did when they were new — $16,562 below the original sticker price of $48,601, on average. That marks the largest dollar gap Edmunds has on record, surpassing the previous high of $16,252 in the last quarter of 2019.

Some 3-year-old models tend to take longer to sell than others, which may give buyers some negotiating power, according to Edmunds. For example, a 3-year-old Jeep Cherokee, with an average transaction price of $23,138 — compared with its original price of $42,263 — takes an average of 64 days to sell, according to Edmunds.

Additionally, a good credit score can help keep the cost of financing a car purchase down. The higher your score, the lower the interest rate you can get, generally speaking.

And, finally, if you're trading in a car, be sure to shop it around, Drury said.

"Get multiple offers. They can vary by thousands of dollars," Drury said. "Have those offers in hand when you're at the dealership that has the next car you want to buy."

#119. Why is there a shortage of 'wizard' elevator mechanics?

Jim Lembesis has been working on elevators in Chicago for 47 years

Some of them are angry. Nerves are frayed. Many of them are just relieved that he's here.

When a lift stops working in Chicago, frazzled building managers might get a visit from Jim Lembesis. For more than 47 years, Lembesis has repaired and upgraded lifts. He says he understands why staff and residents are so anxious to have things working again.

"They wait and they wait," says Lembesis, a field technician for Parkway Elevators. "There's a lot of frustration. Sometimes it's a repeat issue. It's a never-ending cycle, you know what I mean?"

The widely reported shortage of lift and escalator mechanics in the US, which is also a problem in many other countries, has got worse over time, he adds: "It's been like night and day compared to when I started."

Lift and escalator technicians "amaze" with their ability to fix things

Everyone needs a lift now and again. Some people depend on them â elderly residents of high-rise buildings, say, or wheelchair users.

Storm-damaged lifts in Hawaiian apartment blocks this year left some residents trapped, external in their homes for weeks. A lack of mechanics, and problems sourcing parts, were blamed for the repair delays.

In the US, the average salary for a lift mechanic, external is $110,000 (Â£83,000). And yet people in the industry say it is difficult to attract new workers.

Sure, says Lembesis, you do encounter some dangers in a job like this. There are risks of falling from a great height â or being electrocuted. Once, he was even hit on the head with an umbrella by an elderly lady after he rescued her from a stuck lift.

But there are so many skills to learn, and great camaraderie and knowledge-sharing among mechanics, he says. As one recruiter puts it, these technicians amaze people with their ability to fix things. They are like "wizards". The overall-clad shamans who know how to get modern life running again.

Nine times out of 10, the problem is with the doors, says Lembesis. These components repeatedly move back and forth. "People hit them with carts, and dirt and debris [falls into them]," says Lembesis. "[The doors] take the most beating."

Mechanics often keep various spare door parts in their vehicles, ready to use.

But technicians like Lembesis don't just have to get lifts working, they also have to verify the lifts are safe. He remembers one case, decades ago, getting called out to a burning building. His role was to ensure the lifts, which were being drenched with huge volumes of water, were still safe to use while firefighters tackled the blaze.

"I was there for 15 hours that night," he says. "Everyone, on the way out of the door, shook my hand."

In the US there's a four-year apprenticeship to become a lift technician

Finding good lift technicians is "becoming more difficult" says Matt Hollinshead, president of the US National Association for Elevator Contractors. "We have a four-year apprenticeship," he says. That's the first hurdle. And many states require certification or licensing before you can work on lifts.

"We have a vast array of equipment that has to be maintained in the United States." Some lifts are 100-years-old. As time goes on, the technological knowledge required to repair them only grows.

Hollinshead says that weeks or months-long delays to lift repairs are not typically due to staff shortages but that, in such cases, it's likely that a particular part isn't readily available. Manufacturing a replacement hydraulic cylinder takes six to 12 weeks, he notes.

But the lack of technicians does mean that that building managers might have to wait days before someone can even diagnose what's wrong with a lift after it stops working.

Hollinshead says teaching the basics of the trade in high schools or technical schools could entice more people into the role.

Matt Allred, a recruiter and host of The Elevator Careers Podcast, emphasises the many skills that lift mechanics require. "You're a little bit of a wizard, right? You show up and if you can fix it â 'Wow!'."

He blames major lift companies, in part, for locking down maintenance contracts, making it difficult or impossible for third-party repairers to intervene when a lift fails.

Two large lift manufacturers approached by BBC News declined interviews. The chief executive of Otis, one of the firms, said in May that her company was struggling to hire enough staff, external to meet demand. The firm also states on its website, external that the shortage of lift mechanics affects "almost every part of the world".

However, a spokesman for Otis claimed its "US talent pipeline" was healthy, and declined an interview.

Otis says it is happy with its supply of mechanics and engineers

Everyone else who spoke to BBC News said hiring was challenging in the US. Allred was recently contacted by a major lift company and asked to help them find a technician. "I was surprised that a major company would reach out to a little recruiting shop," he says, suggesting that this reflects the extent of the labour shortage.

That shortage is also a problem in the UK. "It is bad and getting worse," says Dave Cooper, president of the Chartered Institution of Building Services Engineers, who is also chief executive of a lift and escalator engineering consultancy.

"We've known food delivery companies to refuse to deliver to flats above the ground floor because the lift is out of service."

Offering better tax breaks to companies that train lift mechanics could help ensure that plenty of workers continue to enter the industry, he adds.

Training to be a lift mechanic is "not a quick route to competency â and it requires people to stick at it for the long term" says Karen Slade, head of assessment at the Lift and Escalator Industry Association (Leia).

Continued investment in training and attracting new workers is "essential", according to Leia. There are currently 259 active lift and escalator apprentices in the UK, the body adds.

Back in Chicago, Lembesis is working with two younger mechanics who have been "really good", he says, so there's at least some hope for the future.

He stresses again how rewarding he finds his work. The people for whom he fixes lifts tend to be very appreciative.

"I love that. You get that reaction from the customers," he says. "Without them, you got nothing."

#118. Microdrama apps chase profits as Hollywood moves into short-form, vertical video

Bogdan Nesvit says his mission is simple: "We are essentially building Disney for the 21st century."

Nesvit is the co-founder of microseries app My Drama, built around the latest, bite-sized form of storytelling. Short, serialized "microdrama" videos are created specifically for mobile devices and have generated billions of views this year. They often feature romantic tropes and typically end in cliffhangers to keep viewers hooked.

The category has exploded at a time when traditional media faces waning interest and YouTube now claims more viewing hours than individual streaming platforms.

The microdrama industry is projected to reach $1.5 billion in revenue in the U.S. in 2026, according to research firm Media Partners Asia. In China, where the format originally gained popularity, microdrama revenue surpassed what was made at the box office in 2024.

"It has the potential to evolve into mainstream entertainment and basically become mobile-first video streaming of the size of Netflix," Nesvit said.

Tech companies and startups such as My Drama currently lead the category in metrics like downloads and time spent on the apps.

TikTok's PineDrama is the most popular microdrama app of 2026 so far with 13.2 million U.S. downloads through September, according to Sensor Tower data.

Startups DramaBox and ReelShort beat out entertainment giants Netflix and Disney in average mobile usage time in the U.S. in the third quarter, according to Sensor Tower.

As they see microdramas win over viewers, legacy media companies, Hollywood executives and actors increasingly want in.

Fox Entertainment was one of the earliest Hollywood investors in microdramas. The entertainment studio acquired an equity stake in Holywater Tech, the company behind My Drama, in October 2025.

Fox agreed to produce at least 200 vertical video titles over two years that would be available on the Holywater app. Thus far, hits include dark academia series "Secret Society: Till Blood Do Us Part" and "Kiss of the Pirate King." Individual series have reached tens of millions of views, according to the company.

Nesvit told CNBC in an interview that partnerships like the one between Fox and Holywater benefit studios by providing the infrastructure to publish their short-form videos and get them in front of an audience.

Beyond distributing the videos, Holywater offers advice for what types of content perform best in a short, mobile-first format, according to Nesvit.

"One of the exciting things that we learned when we started talking to studios in Hollywood around two years ago is that all of them are super hungry for consumer data," Nesvit said. "They really want to see how people are interacting with each second of the content."

Nesvit said the company will provide studios with feedback on everything from the length of a camera shot to what color dress a character should wear.

"Our goal is how we can drive audiences from YouTube, from TikTok, from Instagram to our platform, and also create those kinds of synergies [with] legacy media," he said.

Holywater recently adapted Season 3 of Fox's reality TV hit "Farmer Wants a Wife" into a microseries fit for the platform. The company also plans to create a scripted series featuring 30 three-minute episodes inspired by Fox's "Fear Factor: House of Fear."

Holywater currently relies on a combination of subscription fees, which make up about 80% of its revenue, and money from advertisers. Nesvit said the company is also exploring incorporating brand integrations. The company is already profitable, its co-founders said.

Co-founder Anatolii Kasianov said creating the videos is "very cost efficient," with an estimated expense of between $150,000 and $200,000 for an average 70-minute series.

The company's partnerships with content studios, he said, help to bring down customer acquisition costs.

"You attract organically the audience that loves that [intellectual property]," he said.

While Fox saw collaboration as the way into the format, some longtime Hollywood executives are betting they can skip the partnerships and create microdrama platforms in-house.

Jana Winograde, Susan Rovner and Lloyd Braun, who have collectively held leadership roles at NBCUniversal, Showtime, Warner Bros. Discovery and ABC, have jointly founded a microdrama startup called aTwist.

The company, which officially launched in September, has garnered tens of millions of views as of late November across TikTok, Instagram and YouTube, where content lives in addition to the app.

"One of the specialties that we have is really what it means to be a great storyteller," Rovner told CNBC in an interview. "I was president of Warner Bros. Television. I was there for 22 years. During my time there, we had over 18 series go over 100 episodes, so I definitely think we have an expertise in getting people to care about these shows, care about the characters, invest."

"A lot of our competitors' completion rates are very low, their churn rates are very high," Rovner said. "They can often bring people into the store but they can't keep them."

The average churn rate for the most downloaded microdrama apps is nearly 60% so far in 2026, according to Sensor Tower data. The market intelligence firm notes that the number of users lost in each quarter this year has roughly equaled the number of users gained.

ATwist seeks to keep viewers enticed through high-stakes, compelling characters and unique stories. The app has already ordered second seasons of shows and puts a particular emphasis on talent, according to the co-founders.

Jesse Tyler Ferguson, known for his role as Mitchell on "Modern Family," and his husband, Justin Mikita, a Broadway producer, have collaborated on the musical series "Theater Kids vs. Zombies" for aTwist. It premiered on Oct. 15.

Other series that debuted last month included "Honey, I Think Our House Is Haunted" starring Mario Lopez and "Unboxing the Killer Doll" starring Paris Hilton.

In May, aTwist announced an exclusive partnership with Skydance's linear television channel BET to create horizontal microdramas that air first on BET and later stream in vertical format on aTwist's app.

"That brings a lot of awareness to our brand, a lot of marketing," Winograde said. "But it also gives these linear networks the opportunity to get programming at an incredibly low cost that, from what we're seeing, the audience loves."

Winograde suggested aTwist could strike similar partnerships in the future.

Large streaming companies, which have already faced competition from social media apps and YouTube, are exploring ways to incorporate microdramas inside their existing apps.

NBCUniversal's Peacock launched its own vertical video channel within the app this summer that features both original unscripted series and more traditional vertical dramas. NBCUniversal is also eyeing international development by investing in an Indian microdrama platform, EloElo Group's Story TV, earlier this month.

"At NBCUniversal, we see significant opportunity in mobile-first storytelling and emerging formats like microdramas to engage audiences and meet evolving viewing habits," Deepak Arya, executive vice president for corporate strategy and development at NBCUniversal, said in a press release.

Disney+, Paramount+ and Netflix similarly launched vertical channels embedded in their services this year. Notably, the channels are meant to help viewers discover traditional content and so far haven't featured original, vertical-first series.

On top of those media companies, head of Prime Video and Amazon MGM Studios Mike Hopkins told CNBC's Julia Boorstin in an interview last month that the tech company was interested in producing its own microdramas.

Despite the flurry of investment, Hollywood still faces the challenge of how to translate viewing hours into profit, especially for shorter content.

Leading U.S. microdrama app ReelShort posted a net loss of $12 million in 2025 despite bringing in total revenue of $785 million, according to research compiled by Media Partners Asia, or MPA.

The app, which was founded in 2022 by California-based Crazy Maple Studio, currently reaches about 65 million users every month, according to the company's LinkedIn profile.

However, high marketing and content costs have weighed on the company's bottom line. MPA expects ReelShort to reach profitability for the first time this year, with an estimated $40 million in net profit.

Media Partners Asia CEO Vivek Couto told CNBC that acquiring new users is the largest cost, with ReelShort spending an estimated 55% of its revenue in 2025 on marketing.

Couto also noted high content spend, especially since microdrama apps typically require a high volume of content.

"The content is very hit-driven … it wears out fast," Couto said. "The churn rate for this sector is high, so you have to fill that slate all the time, and you have to win back those users."

Correction: A previous version misstated Holywater Tech's full name in first reference. Co-founder Anatolii Kasianov also said expenses run between $150,000 and $200,000 for an average 70-minute series. A previous version misstated the average series length.

#117. EU leaders fail to break $1.7 trillion budget deadlock as political pressure mounts

European leaders failed to break a deadlock over the EU's next long-term budget on Thursday, leaving negotiators under pressure to meet their year-end target before a busy 2027 election cycle adds further complications.

National leaders are divided over the European Union's next long-term budget, which sets spending priorities and limits from 2028 to 2034. It requires unanimous approval from all 27 member states to pass, as well as a majority vote in favor from members of the European Parliament.

Representatives from EU member states met on Wednesday and Thursday to discuss the latest budget proposal from European Council President António Costa, which amounts to a spending plan worth 1.58 trillion euros ($1.73 trillion). It marks a downward revision of around 10% from the original proposals for the bloc's long-term budget — known as the Multiannual Financial Framework, or MFF.

The EU's budget negotiations come against a backdrop of surging government borrowing costs, spiraling inflation and sluggish growth across much of the continent, as well as concerns about rising budget deficits in various EU countries.

Failure to meet the year-end deadline for the budget to be agreed risks deepening the political deadlock at the center of the ongoing talks.

At a summit in Brussels Thursday, budget talks went on for 12 hours, according to a BBC report — with some participants said to have walked out of the meeting as conflicts emerged around spending priorities and aid for Ukraine.

A spokesperson for the European Commission was not available to comment on Thursday's talks.

German Chancellor Friedrich Merz told reporters on Thursday evening that the talks had brought "new movement" towards reducing the overall size of the budget, adding that leaders would reconvene in mid-December to resume talks.

But he also said: "The European Union comprises of 27 member states, not 23 or 24."

Merz, alongside the leaders of Denmark, the Netherlands, Austria, Finland and Sweden, said in September that the original 2-trillion-euro proposal would need to be reduced by "several hundred billion euros."

"We must make choices," they said in a letter addressed to Costa and Irish Taoiseach Micheál Martin, pointing out that their six countries finance almost 40% of all contributions to the budget.

They called for funding to be focused on defense spending, competitiveness and innovation and "the fight against irregular migration."

Costa labeled the revised budget plans "realistic" when he presented them last week, noting that despite the 10.2% reduction in spending the proposals still represented a 120% jump in competitiveness funding and a 232% upswing for defense compared to the current budget.

"We didn't reach an agreement," he said in a statement after Thursday's meeting. "Regarding the global volume of the budget, some member states asked for further reductions, most opposed further cuts. And some even proposed to reverse some cuts."

Some European parliamentarians also appeared unconvinced by the new proposals.

"Parliament is consistent and proposes more investments, [the] Council is inconsistent and proposes cuts," Romanian MEP Siegfried Mureşan said on Wednesday.

"When the Council finally reaches a position, that's when negotiations will start, not when they end. As prime ministers cannot dictate to national parliaments, prime ministers also cannot dictate to the European Parliament to adopt a budget that is too small, and which does not equip Europe for the future."

Elections are set to go ahead in key EU economies including Spain, Poland and Italy over the next 12 months, while France's spring presidential election is at the forefront of regional leaders' minds.

Current front-runner for the French presidency, far-right candidate Marine Le Pen, has previously called for the country to leave the EU and to cut its contributions to the bloc's budget.

Incumbent French President Emmanuel Macron stressed on Thursday that he had limited time left to have his say on EU spending.

"After 31 December, I will no longer be in a position, politically or democratically, to reopen discussions on the budget and bring them to a conclusion, as we will be ​entering a period far too close to the presidential election in France," he told reporters after the talks, according to French publication France 24.

France, the EU's second-biggest economy after Germany, is being tasked with drastically cutting spending to bring its 5.1% national deficit closer to an EU guideline of 3%, while reeling from months of mass protests, riots and strikes over education funding and a cost-of-living crisis.
